I have the honor to transmit herewith a report
showing in some detail the purchases of the South
Manchuria Railway Company since it was reorganized
umder Japanese law in 1907, and to draw attention to
2
some interesting facts brought out by the figures
submitted.
The Company's purohases in the year ended March
AUG 5
31, 1931, dwindled to Yen 19,837,000, as compared
FILED
with Yen 54,501,000 in the preceding year. Although
136
this radical reduction was necessitated to a great
extent by the sharp decrease in the Company's revenue,
it was also due to the policy of strict economy applied
to all lines of business by the Company's last President,
Dr. M. Sengoku, ex-Premier Hamaguchi's appointee. It
is erpected that the current year's purchases will fall
to even lower levels as a result of the constantly de-
olining earnings of the Company.
is for the causes of the falling off of receipts,
the utter weakness of silver exchange may be regarded
as the fundamental cause. The Company's enterprises
ar

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 •
are conducted on a gold yen basis, and the difficulties
of operating profitably in gold currency at this time
in a country where silver is the standard are obvious.
Competition from new Chinese railroads and general
dullness in import and export trade are also affecting
the Company's earnings.
of interest are the large sales of American goods
to the Company in the past, and the present decay of
this business. The proportion of purohases from the
United States to total purchases in the period from
1907 to 1929 was 21%, whereas in 1930 1t was but 62%.
The value of American goods purchased in 1930 was
Yen 1,300,000, the lowest figure since 1914, when the
Company's entire purchases were but Yen 7,800,000.
On the other hand, the increasing use of Japanese and
Manchurian products deserves note.
Purohases of Japanese and Manchurian goods between
1907 and 1929 composed 34% and 27%, respectively, of
the total purchases. In l930 their respective propor-
tions were 45% and 36%. As Manchurian goods consist
largely of rolling stock, track equipment and building
materials manufactured by Japanese companies in Dairen,
most of them subsidiaries of the Railway Company, it
might well be said that 80% of the Company's purchases
nowadays are Japanese.
As regards the prospects in the near future for
inoreased sales of American goods to the South Manchuria
Railway Company and its affiliated and subsidiary organi-
sations, which operate or control every public service
and important manufacturing enterprise in the Japanese
1eased

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
leased territory and railway area, they are not en-
couraging. Representatives of American manufacturers
in Dairen, local Japanese firms for the most part,
state that it is almost futile nowadays to bid for
railway and other public utility contracts, since it
is the policy and practice of the Railway Company to
patronize Japanese industries and buy only such ne-
cessary articles from America and Europe as are not
and cannot be produced in Japan. While this attitude
is natural and to be expeoted, it affords an illustra-
tion of how the commerce of the world at large sooner
or later suffers where any one nation enjoys some
political advantage in another. In this connection,
it is observed that out of the l29 principal suppliers
and approved contraotors of the Railway Company named
in the lists annexed to the enclosed report only three
are not Japanese.
It is requested that the enclosed report be treated
judiciously if used in connection with trade promotion,
inasmuch as the pamphlets upon which it is based, kindly
furnished to the Consulate by the Railway Company, were
stamped "Confidential."
Respectfully yours,
wm. R. Langdon
American Consul.

The South Manchuria Railway Company has been one
of the largest individual buyers of domestic and im-
ported products in China since it came into Japan's
possession as a result of the Russo-Japanese War.
The following tables illustrate in detail the
volums, character, and souroe of supply of the pur-
shases of the Company since 1907, when it was re-
organised umder Japanese law:

Depression affects Company's Purohases.
The radical meduotion in the Company's purchases
in 1950 was necessitated by diminished revenue in all
lines of business. Purchases in the current year
(Apri1 1, 1931 to Maroh 31, 1932) are expected to be
oven further curtailed as a result of continued de-
olining receipts.
No important new enterprises are contemplated this
year, and purchases will be confined to equipment for
maintenance, replacement and ordinary development.
Prooedure in Purohases.
Purchases are usually made in the following manner:
where equipment and supplies for maintenance and ordi-
nary expansion are needed, the technical officials of
the various departments and enterprises make applica-
tions for such articles to the Directorate, whioh after
approval refers them to the Purohasing Department (in
Dairen) for aotion. The Purohasing Department then
draws up the specifications and conditions for purchase,
and commnicates them to local firms and other inquirers
upon request. No advertising of supplies needed is
done, nor are bids publiely invited. Tenders are in
due course submitted by looal firms and contracts awarded,
time of delivery, quality, reputed performance, and other
faetors being taken into consideration as well as price.
after the contract is awarded, bidders may examine their
competitors: bids. Not less than 80% of the Company's
purohases are made in this manner, viz., by the central
Purohasing Department of the Company in Dairen from
Dairem firms after due competitiom.
2

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.97)
● 7 ●
In addition to purchases made by the Purohasing
Department in Dairen, the local offices and detached
enterprises of the Company (hospitals, mines, town
administrations, eto.) are authorized to buy certain
oquipment and stores on the spot. Then too, parti-
cularly in conneotion with new enterprises, the
Company frequently sends its owm experts abroad to
buy necessary plant directly from the manufacturers.
Method of Seouring Company's Business.
The most effective channel of approach to the
business of the South Manchuria Railway Company is
through looal firms. Annexed to this report is a
statement of sales made by local firms to the Company
in the year ended March 31, 1930, which will give some
idea of their relative standing in this business and
of their suitability to represent American manufacturers.
Building Operations of Company.
The South Manchuria Railway Company is charged by
the Japanese Government, which has a controlling in-
terest in it, with the development and municipal ad-
ministration of towms in the Japanese railway area in
South Manchuria, as well as with the erection of public
buildings of social and cultural character in the Kwan-
tung Leased Territory. The Company's expenditures under
this bead,known as "Land Wanagement", in the year ended
March 31, 1930, amounted to Yen 16,760,000; in the
year ended March 31, 1951, to Yen 15,051,000.
The construotion of new buildings and public works
and the upkeep and remodeling of existing structures are
sometimes undertaken direetly by the Company, but more
freeuomtly

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
frequently they are entrusted to approved contractors
after competitive bidding. Annered to this report
is an up-to-date list of such contractors for both
goneral construotion work and special engineering
installations.
NOTE:-
The ourrency of the foregoing and
annexed tables has been left in Yen, as
average exchange rates for the early years
are not known. Exchange, however, has
been steady during the greatest part of
the period covered, i.e., within a point
or two above or below 50 cents U.S.
currency, so that an adequate idea of the
american equivalent of the values shown
may be gained by dividing them by two.

In a second press communique issued this morning
under the same conditions as those referred to in
Consulate's 225, October 19, 10 p.m., the Japanese
delegation accuses China of violation of treaty
obligations in Manchuria particularly as regards
Jananese railway interests.
It is pointed out that the South Manchurian
Railroad and the maintenance of law and order by
Japanese troops have contributed enorrously to the
productivity of this resion in the last twenty years,
during which time moreover Japan has invested there
over one hundred million dollars. part from
operat ing the South Manchurian Railway Japan has
advanced capital for the construction of other Chinese
lines which are managed by Chinese or mixed boards of
directors.
The statement declares that China has continually
sought

by Japan of the status of the South Manchurian Railway
and in the more recent past has condoned attacks of

note specifically mentions nine attacks which have
occurred since June including the death of Nakanura.
The assertion is made thit it is moreover the

SIR:
I have the honor to transmit herewith the general
balance sheet of the South Manchuria Railway Company
for the year ended March 31, 1931, which was presented
at the general meeting of stockholders in Tokyo on June
20, 1931, and to make some comments on the business of
the Company in the year in question.
In explanation of the delay in submitting the re-
port as well as of its incompleteness in comparison
with previous reports on the same subject from this
office, I beg leave to point out that its preparation
JAN 8 0 19321
was held up owing to the failure of the Company up to
date to furnish the customary statistical and similar
material requested of it on June 26. It is understood
that the Company's foreign correspondence is now censored
by the Kwantung Government and that the failure to re-
ceive a prompt reply is due to this censorship.
JAN 2 01932

General

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
General
Like most commercial concerns the world over the
South Manchuria Railway Company did not enjoy its usual
prosperity in its last business year. Its net profit
dropped to Yen 22,000,000 from Yen 46,000,000 in the
preceding year in spite of strict economies in operating
costs and curtailed expansion.
Reduced freight and
passenger traffic resulting from competition from new
Chinese railroads, a sharp falling off of coal sales
due to low silver exchange in some markets and depression
in others, and dull shipping in Dairen (the Company owns
and operates the wharf and harbor facilities) are mainly
responsible for the decline in the Company's earnings.
Yen 35,000,000 were nevertheless expended on capital
account in expanding existing plant, as against Yen
64,000,000 in the previous year, while 8% dividend was
paid on private and 4.3% on government stook.
In order
to pay this dividend, however, the Company used Yen
10,561,000 of special reserve set aside from the preceding
two years' profits and postponed writing off the full amount
of depreciation on the Company's buildings, which gave rise
to a certain amount of criticism in Japan.
The dividends
last year were 11% on private and 5.3% on state stock, in
addition to a special reserve being set aside on dividend
account.
The Company's assets increased from Yen 1,087,000,000
to Yen 1,116,600,000, and its reserves from Yen 174,000,000
to Yen 189,000,000 in the period covered.
Railroad Business

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)

Railroad Business
Net profit from railroad operation amounted to Yen
60,000,000 as compared with Yen 82,000,000 in the pre-
vious year. Traffio was as follows:

was expended, as compared with Yen 19,300,000 in the pre-
vious year.
The main cause of the decline of traffic was com-
petition from the new Chinese railroads in Manchuria,
which are operated on a silver currency basis in con-
trast to the gold yen standard of the South Manchuria
Railway. In view of the low silver exchange situation,
advantage was taken of the Chinese transportation system
wherever possible by both passengers and shippers.
The value of the Company's railroad property is
now valued at Yen 270,000,000, and comprises the follow-

Harbors and Wharves

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Harbors and Wharves
Income from operation of the harbor and wharf faci-
lities of Dairen fell from Yen 12,000,000 in the pre-
ceding year to Yen 8,500,000, the net profit having been
Yen 2,400,000 as compared with Yen 4,500,000 the year
before. Dull shipping resulting from world depression
and,to a lesser extent, from active competition from
Newohwang was responsible for the shrinking of the Com-
pany's profits in this department.
The following shipping figures for Dairen will con-
vey an idea of the cause of the dwindled receipts:
Calendar Years

The magnificent Kanchingtze coaling pier, across
the bay from Dairen, together with its 980-metre break-
water, was completed in the year under review.
This
pier, of steel throughout, is 550 metres long, and is
quipped with four motor steel loaders of 6oo tons per
hour loading capacity each. The pier is connected,
by means of two bridge transporters and six pier cars,
with a coal storage yaru 130,000 square metres in area.
The total cost of the pier and installation, since work
was commenced on it in 1926, was Yen 12,000,000.
Besides completing the Kanohingtze pier, work was
pushed forward on the fourth pier in Dairen and an ex-
tensive

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
tensive area along the shore front reclaimed.
The
total length of berthing quays in Dairen is now 18,000
feet, sufficient to dock 337,000 tons of shipping at one
time. In addition there are, outside of the Dairen
breakwater, the coal pier at Kanchingtze described above
and the inflammable goods pier at Jijiko, with 1,1l6
feet berthing length.
The entire length of these piers
is served with the most up-to-date electric cranes and
mechanical cargo handling equipment, while there are 68
miles of railroad within the wharf compounds to facilitate
the shifting of cargo.
The Company's warehousing facilities on the Dairen
piers and within the Dairen wharf compounu now comprise
75 warehouses with 371,16l square metres of floor space.
Altogether Yen 5,830,000 was expended on account of
harbors and wharves in the year under review, as compared
with some Yen 10,000,000 in the preceding year.
The
Company's harbor and wharf properties, including those
at Antung, Yingkow (Newchwang), Port Arthur and Shanghai
are now valued at Yen 83,000,000.
Collieries

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)

Collieries
The exploitation of the Company's coal mining con-
oessions at Fushun and Yentai netted a profit of Yen
4,600,000, as compared with Yen 16,000,000 the year
before. A smaller volume of sales and reduced prices
to meet low silver exchange in the main markets, Man-
churia and Central and South China, were the main causes
of the sharp drop in profits fron coal mining.
The output of coal and principal by-products at the

Shale Oil

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
Shale Oil Distillation Plant
The long contemplated scheme of utilizing the oil
shale above the coal bed at Fushun materialized in 1928,
when the construction of a distillation plant was begun.
This plant was completed in November l929 at a cost of
Yen 9,000,000, and its output since then has been as
follows:
Dec.1929/Mar.1930
Year Ended March 31, 1931
Shale oil
3,987 metric tons

7,800 long
“
The annual capacity of the plant is 70,000 tons of
fuel oil, 18,000 tons of sulphate of anmonia, and 15,000
tons of crude paraffin, so that production last year was
well below capacity, as was also that of paraffin.
Low
world prices of these conmodities made the manufacture of
a greater quantity unpractical.
The profit from operation of this enterprise was
Yen 300,000.
The shale oil is sold exclusively to the
Japanese Navy, while the paraffin is sent to the Company's
affiliate the Japan Paraffin Ma facturing Company, Limited,
at Tokuyama, Japan (Capital Yen 2,O00,O00), for refining.

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.98)

Blast Furnaces
The net profit from iron and by-products manufac-
tured at the Conpany's blast furnaces at Anshan amounted
to Yen 76,000, as compared with Yen 1,200,000 in the pre-
vious year.
This would appear to be a paper profit, how-
ever, inasauch as the President of the Company, referring
to iron manufacture at the annual stockholders' meeting,
stated that the operation of this department caused the
Company a loss of Yen 660,000.
The decline in profits
from this source was due to a weak demand and prevailing
low pig iron prices in Japan nd other markets.

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)

Looal Administration and Exploitation
The munioipal administration and development of
Japanese railway towns and town sites in South Man-
churia cost the Company Yen 10,500,000, approximately
Yen 3,000,000 less than the previous year.
Total ex-
penditure under this head amounted to Yen 15,100,000,
as compared with Yen 18,000,000 the year before.
Rates, taxes and other revenue collected from residents
of these areas was Yen 4,500,000, which is slightly be-
low the 1929/30 collections.
Of the Yen 15,000,000 spent in this connection,
some Yen 3,000,000 went for new installations,sohools
and hospitals, and the balance for maintenance and
general munioipal improvement.
It is evident that, regardless of bad business and
shrinking revenue, the Company's policy of vigorously
building up its lands anà towns underwent no change.
Whereas drastic reductions were made in every other de-
partment of business, expenditure for town development
was on practically the same scale as in the boom year
1929. It is also interesting to note that the contri-
bution of the residents of railway settlements toward
their government and publio benefits remains more or
less stationary in spite of the increase in their
numbers.
The following figures illustrate the Company's
polioy of intensive developnent of Japanese town sites in
South Manchuria:

Business

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.98)
-15-
Business Prospects in 1931/32
The business of the Company has showed no improve-
ment in the current year. In fact, it has grown much
worse, and radical retrenchment in operating expendi-
ture, capital investment, and industrial encouragement
has been necessary.
Among the more drastic measures
to lower operating costs may be cited the discharge in
August of 2, 600 staff eaployees and the curtailment of
In 1928/29 the
purchases of supplies and equipment.
Company bought Yen 54,000,000 worth of goods for its
different enterprises.
This figure dropped to Yen
19,000,000 in 1929/30, and this year is expected to
fall below Yen 12,000,000. It has also been necessary
in the interests of economy to merge certain of the
affiliated companies, the absorption of the Manchuria
Dock Company by the Dairen Steansip Company (Dairen
Kisen Kaisha) having been the most i portant step in
this direction thus far.
The shrinking of profits is due mainly to dull
passenger and freight traffic and to reduced coal sales.
Revenue from railroad operation in the first six months
(April 1 to September 30) of the year was as follows:
(Apr/Sept.1930)

Figures

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
-16•
Figures covering the Company's coal business in the
first half of the current year are not available, but
exports of coal from Dairen (consisting of the Company's
products exolusively) indicate that the physical volume
of sales has kept up satisfactorily.
Prices have been
lowered to such a degree, however, to meet competition
from Indian coal in South China and Chinese-mined coal
in North China and Manchuria that the profit has been
below that of last year.
Harbor and wharf receipts
have also fallen below those of a year ago.
As there appear to be no encouraging factors in the
immediate business prospects of the Conpany, it is esti-
mated that the net profit for the current year will be
about half of what it was last year, which may necessi-
tate the suspension of a dividend on state stock.
Looking into the causes of the dullness of the Com-
pany's business, it is found that they are, in the main,
the decline in passenger traffic, smaller profits fron
freight transportation and coal sales, which have nain-
tained their normal volume, and diminished collections
from harbor and wharf operation in Dairen.
Passenger traffic has fallen off owing to the sub-
siding of the tide of immigration into Manchuria and to
competition from Chinese lines.
Freight profits have
declined mainly because of competition from the Chinese
railroad system, which have necessitated successive re-
duotions in freight rates.
Harbor revenues are at a
low ebb on account of dull shipping resulting from both
world depression and from competition from Newohwang,
the temporary seaport of the Chinese railway system.
Coal

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.97)
 17 -
Coal sales are unprofitable because of low gold yen
value of silver and competition. Thus competition
rather than economic depression appears to be the
principal cause of the poor state of the Company's
business. It is believed that a difficult future
confronts the Company if competition from Chinese
railroads and harbors continues without restriction.
Respectfully yours,
AmRLanga1
Wm. R. Langdon,

TOKYO,JAPAN
PYYO.77
Ihave the honor to refer to my despatch No. 232
of Ootober 20, l931, reviewing the business of the
813
South Manchuria Railway Company in the year ended March
31, l93l, and to go into further detail regarding com-
petition from Chinese railroads and harbors, which was
held to be the main cause of the decline of the Company's
business and which it is believed lies at the bottom of
the present Sino-Japanese conflict.
Not much is heard of Chinese competition in polem-
ical literature regarding Manchuria, but it neverthelegs
remains a fact that Chinese railroads and Newchwang hae
FILHD
bitten deep into the profits of the South Manchuria
1932
Railway Company this year.
The prosperity of the competitive Chinese railroads
as compared with the unparalleled dullness which has be-
set the Japanese lines is indicated in the following
Japanese account of their earnings for the first half
of this year:
Japanese

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
Rate of increase or decrease
compared with previous year
Japanese Lines:
South Manchuria Railway . . . .
19% decrease
Chinchow-Pitsuwo Railway
37%
n
Chinese Lines:
Ssupingkai-Taonan Line
50% increase
Mukden-Hailung Line
30%
88/8
Kirin-Hailung Line
110%
Taonan-Anganchi Line
58%
The rate of increase of the Newchwang-Mukden and
Tahushan-Tungliao branches of the Peking-Mukden Rail-
way, which carry the freight of the above lines to the
seaboard, is not known, but it is obvious that it must
correspond with that of their above-listed feeders.
It is believed that the future holds little pro-
mise of prosperity for the South Manchuria Railway as
long as competition from Chinese lines continues un-
restricted. As an example of the situation con-
fronting the Japanese, the case of beans, the most im-
portant and best paying cargo offered to railroads in
Manchuria, may be taken. The Chinese carload rate on
beans from North Manchuria to Newchwang is U.S.$150
less than the through Harbin-Dairen carload rate on the
Japanese line! The profits of the Chinese lines in-
dicate that even with this low rate there is a margin
for lowering charges, whereas the Japanese, with their
heavy capital investment and meager profits, appear to
have no room left for downward revisions of their tariff.
Although tonnage figures for Newchwang with which
to form an aocurate idea of the relative progress of the
port this season are not yet obtainable, there are nu-
merous indications that this makeshift seaport is un-
usually active. This Consulate is charged with the
preparation

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
preparation of a monthly survey of the Manchurian soy-
bean market, and it is an interesting development that
whereas in previous years the movement of beans and
bean products from Vladivostok and Dairen alone fi-
gured in official market reports, this year Newchwang
exports receive regular attention. It is also a fact
that a large number of Dairen bean crushers have moved
to Newchwang in the past twelve months to take advan-
tage of the low freight charges on raw material for
their mills.
Using exports of beans and bean products as an
indicator of the progress of Newchwang, it is noted
that the exports of these commodities for the entire
boom year 1929 amounted to 260,000 tons, whereas in
six months from April, when the port was free from
ice, to September of this year, they totalled 430,000
tons in spite of economic depression. The relative
importance of Newchwang in the soybean trade of Man-
churia since last April is shown in the following
table:
Combined bean, beancake, and beanoil exports
April - September, 1931
From Newohwang
430,000 tons
Vladivostok. .
677,000
18
Dairen
.1,209,256
As regards the physical volume of import trade into
Newohwang, figures are not yet obtainable, but it is un-
derstood that it has increased considerably since April
lst of this year, when Dairen was isolated from domestic
and transit foreign trade by customs legislation (vide
my despatch No. 200 of June 9, 1931). It is observed
694.93251
from

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
from this Consulate's monthly surveys of the South Man-
churia flour market, for instance, that wheat flour im-
ports in July and August were as follows:
Dairen . . . . . 768,000 bags
Newohwang. . . 628,000
When it is considered that in six months of through
rail service and with a poor outlet like Newohwang traf-
fio in the most paying cargo on the Chinese railroad
system has risen to approximately one-third of the same
traffic on the South Manchuria Railway, it can only be
concluded that the Chinese share in the carrying trade
of Manchuria will increase when the Chinese system is
enlarged, improved and linked with the deep, ice-free
port of Hulutao under construction.
Can the South Manchuria Railway Company afford to
lose any more ground to the Chinese transportation
system?
Its earnings last year necessitated curtail-
ment of capital investment, postponement of pending
projects, and suspension of aid to Japanese enterprise
in Manchuria, in addition to a cut in the rate of divi-
dend.
This year, with revenue probably less than half
that of last year, there will have to be still greater
retrenchment and a very small, if any, dividend.
fact, a Tokyo news item a few days ago mentioned the
likelihood of using the Company's reserves for payment
of dividend. It is obvious that expansion, industrial
and
projects,/empire-building schemes will have to be
abandoned if earnings do not rise above the level of
the past six months.
The Japanese have been seeking to reach agreements
with

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
● 5 •
with the Chinese railroads looking to mutually profit-
able cooperation and division of traffic.
The Japanese
efforts have failed for obvious reasons.
The Chinese
can see no reason for sharing their big profits with the
Japanese, who have not shovn any great consideration for
their interests in the past.
There is the political
motive too for refusal to cooperate.
Will Japan remain a passive onlooker of the decay
of her investment in Manchuria?
The double-tracking
of the main line from Dairen to Changchun is almost
finished; a gigantic coal pier equipped with the latest
mechanical devices has just been completed in Dairen Bay;
work is proceeding on a fourth pier in Dairen; on every
hand are evidences of a forward policy, of preparation
for a great future.
The answer to the question was given on September
18, when the Japanese army occupied strategic railroad
centers in South Manchuria and overthrew the government
that was pushing forward the competitive programme.
Reports to the League of Nations indicate that since
that date Japanese troops severed the Chinese railroad
system in such a manner that the four Chinese lines
mentioned early in this despatch are now feeding the
South Manchuria rather than the Peking-Mukden Railway.
It is firmly believed that the present Sino-
Japanese conflict will not be settled until the South
Manchuria Railway and the port of Dairen are adequately
proteoted from Chinese competition, either by a con-
firmation of existing agreements on this question or
by the conolusion of a new agreement.

Dairon, Maroh 24th, 1953.
R: CHINCHOW-PITZUWO (CHENGTZUTUNG) RAILAY
(IN KWANTUNG LEASKD TERRITORY)
Note: This railway is not and has never been under
the managament of the South Manchuria Railway Co., al-
though the Chinohow-Pitzuwo line has been subsidized by
the S.M.R. Co.
Chinahow-Pitzuwo (Chengtzutung) Railway
Distanoe:
102.2 kilometres
Construoted:
May, 1926, and completed 0ct. 1927
Capi tal
Yen 4,000,000
Paid up:
Yen 2,000,000
Debenture:
Yen 2,000,000
This line was construoted for the purpose of opening
up the mertheastern area of the Kwantung Leased Territory
whioh oontains in parts good soil for agriculture, ex-
cellent sites for salt fields and huge granite and other
stome deposits for building materials. Prior to the
construetion of this railway the region was somewhat
dormant and trade was carried ohiefly by junks.
After l927, Pitzuwo became a flouriahing town, the
farmers along the line inoreased their plantings, es-
pecially peanuts; salt fields were laid out on more
modern lines; and stone quarries were established.
The South Manohuria Rail way Co., however, gave
assistance to the Chinohow-Pitzuwo line in the form of
a mubsidy to last for a period of five years, from the
time the line was conatruoted. This amounted to Yen
559,000.
The new line, responsible for opening up the north-
eastern part of the Kwantung Leased Territory, failed at
the start and the subsidy furnished by the S.M.R. Co.
were appropriated for the dividend payment with the re-
sult that the total amount of Yen 559, 000 was used 1n
four instead of five years.
Business, partly on account of the world depression,
did mot impeeve, and as a result the management, therefore,
applied during the latter part of 1931 to the S.M.R. Co.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.97)
一 8 -
for the continuamce and increase of subsidies. The
applieation was rejected by the S..R. Co. on the
groumd that the Chinohow-Pitzuwo line is of more signi-
fieance in the maintenance of peace and order in the
region than in the econonie value. It was thought that
this lime should obtain finanoial aid from the Kwantung
Governmemt.
The situation sinoe l93l has undergone a change
and the S.M.R. saw the need of assisting the line in the
spirit of enoouraging private en terprises. The region
through which the line passes has not yet been fully
developed of its natural resources, and although there
are no new plans for development yet, this line 1s con-
sidered important and hopes are entertained that the
Chinohow-Pitzuwo railway will soon be able to show a
profit.
The details, such as the amount of the subsidy and
the duration will be decided later.
It may be noted that the hinohow-ritzuwo line has
no rolling stoak of its owm. Locomotives (inoluding
the engineers), freight and passenger cars are rented
from the S.M.R. Co.
Press reports alleging that the Chinohow-Pitzuwo
(Chengtzutung) line will be extended northeasterly to
Antung are unfounded. The area from Chengtzutung to
Antung is mountainous and would involve a tremendous
expense, since several tunnels would have to be con-
struoted. The distance fron ntung to Dairen would,
it is true, be shortened considerably but it is doubtful
whether this route would be commeroially profitable for
a railway.

Subject: Militarization of the South Manchuria Railway.

894.00
ONI
82
COPIES SENT TO
The Honorable
O.N.I. AND M. I. D.
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
Recently the Japanese Army authorities have revived
and elaborated a scheme for placing the South Manchuria
Railway under military control, which was first proposed
EC  1933
in the spring of 1932 but temporarily abandoned. Th
893.01
352
Embassy's despatch No. 22 of June 18, 1932, described
the confusion of Japanese authority in Manchuria and the
Kwantung Leased Territory resulting from partition of
this authority under four heads; the Kwantung Garrison
Commander, the Kwantung Civil Governor, the South Man-
churia Railway Governor, and the Consul General at Mukden.
After

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
After the recognition of "Manchukuo" as an independent
state, this division of authority was partially solved
by placing all Japanese authority under the "Ambassador
to Manchukuo", who is at the same time commander of the
Kwantung Army. Unification of political administration
was thus assured, but the South Manchuria Railway, being
primarily a business concern with half its capital in
the hands of shareholders and subject to direction by the
Department of Overseas Affairs in Tokyo, has remained
outside of direct Army control.
The present plan contemplated by the Army, which
goes far beyond the original scheme, calls for the dis-
solution of the South Manchuria Railway Company and its
reorganization as a great holding company for the mani-
fold enterprises now operated by the company, and for the
retrocession of the railway zone to "Manchukuo". It
would place direction of the holding company under the
"Ambassador", and consequently transfer authority over the
railway from the Department of Overseas Affairs to the Army.
The direction of the holding company would be in the hands
of a bureau of the Kwantung Army to be organized by a merger
of the existing economic section of the Kwantung Army and
the existing research department of the Railway. Of the
separated industries, those vitally related to national
defence, such as coal, iron, etc., would also be placed under
the Economic Section of the Kwantung Army. The Railway
proper would be conducted as a joint Japanese-"Manchukuo"
enterprise by the amalgamation of the Railway Department
of the South Manchuria Railway and "Mancnukuo" Railways
Department which the South Manchuria Railway company now
manages

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
manages in trust. Presumably the remaining industries
would be operated as ordinary business enterprises under
the supervision of the "Ambassador" through the holding
company.
These proposals have not as yet been formally made to
the Cabinet, but have been widely discussed in the press
and have been commented on by various officials in the
Government.
The press accounts vary to some extent in
describing these proposals, but they all agree in pointing
to an effort of the Army to obtain control over the Railway.
It is clear that the most important aim of the proposals
is to separate the railway from the supervisory control of
the Overseas Affairs Department in Tokyo, and to place it
under the authority of the Kwantung Army.
The contention of the Army in proposing this reor-
ganization is that the existing administrative set-up in
Manchuria effected last Spring, is still unsatisfactory,
and that national defence policies as well as the economic
development of Manchuria demand a more unified authority.
Behind the Army's contention is the commonly known fact
that the South Manchurian Railway has suffered frequently
in the past from interference in personnel and policies by
the political parties in Japan. The Railway has been forced
to contribute large sums to the parties and has long been
a political football.
These abuses the Army would prevent, but more important
is the Army's determination, not publicly expressed but
generally

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
generally believed by observers, to organize the South
Manchuria Railway under military control in preparation
for a war with Russia. The existing administrative con-
trol, divided as it is between Tokyo, Dairen and Hsinking,
is far too cumbersome from the military viewpoint in case
of an emergency.
It seems likely that this proposed reorganization
is in line with other proposals sponsored by General Araki
and discussed somewhat vaguely in the press, calling for
far-reaching reorganizations in Japan affecting industry,
education, finance, agriculture and other matters con-
cerned with national defence. It is significant that
General Hishikari, "Ambassador to Manchukuo", informed
a prominent American observer that the proposal for reor-
ganization of the South Manchuria Railway is part of the
program of nationalization, or militarization of industry
to be adopted first in Manchuria. If successful there,
he stated, it will be extended to Japan where because of
precedent and prejudice it will be harder to establish.
The most perplexing of the Army's pronosals is that
calling for retrocession of the Railway zone to "Manchukuo".
The only possible explanation is that the Army envisages
permanent control over "Manchukuo" with eventual annexation,
in which case the zone would be superfluous. My informant
above mentioned, states that General Hishikari has a most
grandiose vision of the future of the new state; that it is
destined to become an integral part of a bloc with Japan,
and that Manchuria would some day be the center of authority
and power of the Yamato race, overshadowing Japan itself.
Only

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Only on behalf of this vision of "manifest destiny" would
the Army be likely deliberately to propose the retro-
cession of this important treaty right.
The reaction of the civil authorities and the public
to these proposals by the Army seems not at all favorable.
The need for further unification of authority over the South
Manchuria Railway is generally conceded, but the Army's
plan is deemed too revolutionary. The Department of Over-
seas Affairs, naturally jealous of its own authority, is
reported to have objected on the score that the financial
and economic interests involved are so vast that reorgani-
zation according to the Army's plan is imnossible. Half
of the Yen 8,000,000,000 capital of the Railway is owmed by
private shareholders as are also a huge amount of debentures.
To effect the proposed reorganization would entail liquida-
tion of these private interests, impossible under resent
conditions of national finance. Other objections are that
treaty obligations with "Manchukuo" are involved which if
changed would cause difficulties, and that "Manchukuo" is
financially unable to assume the obligations attendant to
taking over the detached industries.
Industrial leaders in Japan are said to be hostile to
the Army's proposals because of the possible effect on Japa-
nese industry of military control of those detached indus-
gries related to national defence, mainly iron and coal
production. The Army has some very original ideas about
the building up of a "happy valley" in Manchuria which do
not please business men. If the Army undertakes the develop-
ment of iron, coal, etc. in Manchuria, these products will
1nevitably

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
inevitably compete with domestic industry. The press
reported that opposition to the proposed changes was
expressed in a draft resolution drawn up by the Japan
Chamber of Commerce on November 13th. The statement
urged that if reorganization is required, the matter
should be considered by all those who are concerned. Other
opinion observed in the press opposes the proposals on the
score that authority over the Railway lies out of the scope
of military affairs and that soldiers have no business con-
cerning themselves with onomic matters.
The OSAKA MAINICHI reported on November 8 that inasmuch
as foreign interests are concernea with the proposed changes,
the Foreign Office will shortly issue a statement to reassure
the Powers that the plan to reorganize the South Manchuria
Railway will not affect in any way the principle of the
Open Door in Manchuria. As yet no such statement has ap-
peared, nor is any such statement likely until the pro-
posals have formally been made to the Cabinet.
When these proposals are formally presented, the
Cabinet will find itself in a serious predicament. It
obviously cannot approve the entire program, but to
reject it would estrange the Army and threaten the stability
of the Cabinet, already showing evidence of cleavage over
the budgetary demands of the Army and Navy. It remains to be
seen how Admiral Saito will steer his Cabinet around this
latest embarrassment devised by an ambitious military.
Respectfully yours,
Joh. Juu
877.

Joseph C. Grew.

singe 1931 the working capital of the co ipany has been

the Fiscal Year, 1934-35."

September 3, 1935.
The attaahed despatch No. 1424 of
August 8, 1935, from the Embassy at Tokyo
disousses the significanoe of the appoint-
ment of Mr. Yosuke Matauoka as Vice-Presi-
dent of the South Manohuria Rallway.
It will be reoalled that Mr. Matsuoka
was sent to Geneva in 1932 to represent
Japan at the meeting of the Assembly of
the League of Mations which was oalled to
take action on the report of the Lytton
Commission on the "logality of Japan's
actions in Manohuria .... although he did
not sueeeed (as probably no man oould have
done) in convinoing the Assembly of the
justice of Japan's aotions, he presented
Japan's case in a forceful and loquent
manner whioh attraoted wide attention".
The Bebassy observes that the appoint-
mont of Mr. Matsuoka to his present position
at this time has special significance.
Count Hayashi, who resigned as Pres ident of
the Railway, was belioved not to be in
sympathy either with the Japanese military
in Chine, who expeot to use the South
Manchuria Railway in exploiting North
China, nor with the Yinister of Pinance,
who does not approve of further investment
abroad at this time of Japanese capital.
Mr. Matsuoka, who is an oxtrome nationalist,
1.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 8 -
1s popular with the Japanese army and with
patriotie socleties, of which there are a
large mumber. The Embassy believes, there-
fore, that the appointment of Kr. Matauoka
was diotated by the need of having in con-
trol of the Rallway one who would oooperate
with the Japanese army in its projocts.
The Bmbassy cites a number of state-
mente in the Japanese press indicating that
there will shortly be initiated a "move to

SUBJECT:
THE APPOINTMENT OF MR. YOSUKE MATSUOKA AS
PRESIDENT OF THE SOUTH MANCHURIA RAILWAY.

SRY
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
SEP 24 1935
Sir:
FILED
I have the honor to report that for some time
past there have been vague rumors of differences of
opinion between the Kwantung Army and the management
of the South Manchuria Railway, which culminated in
CF
the resignation on August 2, 1935, of the President
of the Railway, Count Hirotaro Hayashi, who had held
the post for three years. According to RENGO, Count
Hayashi
F

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.97)
129
-2-
Hayashi was not in agreement with the Kwantung Army
nor with some members of the staff of the Railway,
including the Vice-President, Mr. Hatta. Mr. Hatta,
it appears, was in close touch with the Kwantung Army
and carried on all negotiations with the Army, with
the result that Count Hayashi was relegated to the
background. Count Hayashi also, according to some of
the Japanese newspapers, did not agree with General
2in
Minami, the Japanese Ambassador to "Manchukuo", in
regard to the role to be played by the South Manchuria
Railway in the exploitation of North China and did not
agree with Mr. Takahashi, the Minister of Finance, in
regard to the financing of further ventures of the
Railway. No official reason for Count Hayashi's resig-
nation was issued, but Count Hayashi himself is reported
by the YOMIURI to have explained it on the grounds that
a new situation had been created in North China, as a
result of which the South Manchuria Railway had many
things to accomplish from a national point of view,
and Count Hayashi wished to open the way for a younger
へ
man.
On the day of the acceptance of Count Hayashi's
resignation, Mr. Yosuke Matsuoka was appointed President
of the South Manchuria Railway by the Cabinet, with
Imperial sanction. Mr. Matsuoka, who is now fifty-five
years of age, was educated in the United States, at
Oregon Law College. He entered the Japanese diplomatic
service

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
service and rose to the rank of Consul General, but,
dissatisfied with the slowmess of advancement, he
resigned and entered the service of the South Manchuria
Railway. He rose to the position of Director, and in
1928, when Mr. Jotaro Yamamoto was appointed President
of the Railway, Mr. Matsuoka was selected as his Vice
President. Losing this position as a result of a
political change, Mr. Matsuoka entered domestic politics
and was twice elected to the Lower House of the Diet
as a Seiyukai member. In 1933 he resigned from the
Diet and the Seiyukai, and organized the Seito Kaisho
Remmei (Society for the Dissolution of Political Parties),
which aimed to do away entirely with political parties
and thereby bring about the "Showa Revolution", by
which all pover would be returned to the Throne.
Mr. Matsuoka is perhaps best known to the outside
world, however, through his efforts at the meeting of
the League Assembly in 1932, when the legality of
Japan's actions in Manchuria was under consideration.
Mr. Matsuoka was the chief Japanese delegate and, al-
though he did not succeed (as probably no man could
have done) in convincing the Assembly of the justice
of Japan's actions, he presented Japan's case in a
forceful and eloquent manner which attracted wide at-
tention.
Mr. Matsuoka is a man of wide experience, con-
siderable political ability, great energy and great
force

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.98)
131
-4-
force of character. He also possesses to some extent
that peculiar idealistic vision of which General Araki
was the great proponent, but, unlike General Araki,
Mr. Matsuoka keeps his feet on the ground as well as
 his head in the clouds. Because of his force of
character, his intense patriotism, his belief in the
destiny of the Japanese people, and his idealistic
vision, Mr. Matsuoka is popular with the Japanese Army
and with the numerous patriotic societies.
Although changes in the head personnel of the
South Manchuria Railway have taken place frequently
in the past and have aroused little comment, it is
generally felt that the appointment of Mr. Matsuoka
to the position of President at this particular
moment has some special significance. The principal
purpose of this despatch, therefore, is to point out
the significance of the appointment, as seen by the
Embassy.
The obvious purpose of the appointment of Mr.
Matsuoka to the position of President of the South
Manchuria Railway was to place in the position someone
who would not be in conflict with the authorities of
the Kwantung Army, as Count Hayashi had been, and who
would cooperate with the Army in its projects. The
question arises, however, as to the especial necessity
which called for the change at this particular time.
The

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.98)
132
-5-
The OSAKA MAINICHI, English Edition, of August 7,
1935, lists the unsettled problems faced by the new
head of the South Manchuria Railway as follows:
1. Reorganization of the business system of the
Railway. This problem involves the question of the
operation of subsidiary enterprises, such as the Fushun
Colliery, by the Railway. There has been considerable
agitation in recent years for the detachment of these
enterprises from the railway proper, in order that the
railway may devote itself solely to the operation of
rail lines.
2. Reorganization of railway enterprises in
Manchuria. This problem involves the standardization
of the railway lines in Manchuria, the centralization
of control (at present vested in the South Manchuria
Railway, the "Manchukuo" Railway Administration Board
and the North Chosen Railway Administration Board
presumably operating the railways from the Manchuria
border to the new northern Korean ports on the Japan
Sea), and the standardization of the valuation of
railway properties.
3. Reorganization of personnel. Because of past
conflict of opinions as to policy, the staff of the
Railway is reported to have failed to work always as a
perfect unit, and shifts in personnel are necessary.
4. Economic activities of the South Manchuria
Railway in China. This problem involves the making of
the

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.97)
15
-6-
the necessary investigations, decisions as to enter-
prises to be undertaken, and the raising of the neces-
sary capital.
7
Although no official statement has been made on
the subject, it is the belief of many observers of the
situation, including the foreign newspaper correspondents,
that the fourth problem given above, namely, the economic
exploitation of North China by the Japanese, is the
basic factor causing the appointment of Mr. Matsuoka as
President of the Railway at this time.
Since the recent North China incident, as a result
of which the anti-Japanese elements in North China were
largely eliminated and the Japanese military obtained
a measure of political control over the region, reports
have appeared from time to time in the newspapers in
regard to the projected Japanese exploitation of the
natural resources and means of communication in North
China. Reports have also appeared to the effect that
the Kwantung Army intends to use the South Manchuria
Railway as the spear-head in this economic penetration
of China. Count Hayashi was a man of moderate views,
a good executive of the Railway, but hardly the man to
carry out the "positive" policies of the Kwantung Army.
Hence the appointment of Mr. Matsuoka, who is a man
vastly better suited to the work which the Kwantung Army
This view of the basic cause of the change in
presidents of the Railway is borne out by various hints
1n

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
13
-7-
in the newspapers. The TOKYO NICHI-NICHI on August 3
stated that, under Mr. Matsuoka, the South Manchuria
Railway and the Army will work together in trying to
formulate a sound plan for investment in North China,
"taking into consideration the economic interests of
Great Britain, the United States and the Soviet Union
in that region". On August 2, General Minami, in an
interview given newspaper men in Hsinking, stated, in
reply to a question as to his opinions in regard to
the projected move to secure Sino-Japanese economic
rapprochement through the South Manchuria Railway,
that he believed that not only North China but all
China could be included in such a move. The CHUGAI
SHOGYO on August 2 commented editorially
"The mission of the railway concern has been
enlarged with the recent solution of the North
China situation and the consequent need to
develop that region economically. . . Man-
chukuo has gone through a transitory period
and has now entered a new period of large-
scale industrial construction. Manchukuos
future economic relations must also be
considered. In these respects, the head
of the company (the South Manchuria Rail-
way) must be an able person. The change
of the presidential post at this moment
is natural."
The TOKYO ASAHI on August 2 also commented editorially
along the same line:
"The situation in North China, contiguous
to Manchukuo, has been brought to a satis-
factory settlement, and the ground prepared
for an economic rapprochement between Japan,
Manchukuo and North China. The activities
of the South Manchuria Railway, as the center
of such cooperation, are attracting atten-
tion. The weighty task set itself by the
railway makes it necessary for the concern
to use every care in the formulation of its
course

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
135
-8-
course, especially in its policy in North
China. . . . We expect much of the leader-
ship of Mr. Matsuoka, who is intimately
acquainted with conditions in North China."
Mr. Matsuoka himself, in a statement which he made to
the members of his Society for the Dissolution of the
Political Parties on August 4, implied that his prin-
cipal task as President of the South Manchuria Railway
was the penetration of North China. His statement,
which also gives some idea of the visionary character
of the man, reads in part as follows (JAPAN ADVER-
TISER translation):
"Looking over the northeastern area of
the Asiatic mainland, we see that Manchuria
and Mongolia have already passed through
the first stage, that of reshaping, and are
entering on the second stage, that of in-
ternal perfection. Because of the activities
of the Soviet Union and the situation pre-
vailing in China, Japan is going to start
operations in North China. Most of the people
of Japan do not yet quite understand the great
importance of these future operations, and
their lack of understanding, I believe, will
beyond doubt bring about a really serious
crisis in the nation. Regardless of how
serious the crisis may become, Japan cannot
halt its North China operations. The arrow
has already left the bow. The progress of
these operations will decide the destiny of
the Yamato race, its rise or fall in the
world situation.
"To carry through these operations,
domestic renovation is inevitable. It is for
this reason that I am championing the cause
of the Showa Restoration. I am going to
Manchukuo and shall take leave of you for a
while. The true spirit of the movement for
dissolution of the political parties is in
no sense connected with politics. It is a
spiritual movement."
The appointment of Mr. Matsuoka to the position of
President of the South Manchuria Railway was received
with approval in nearly all circles in Japan and Manchuria.
AS

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
136
-9-
As was stated before, Mr. Matsuoka is popular with the
Army - in fact, it is reported that he was recommended
for the post by General Minami - while he is in sympathy
with the ultra-patriotic and reactionary societies. His
appointment therefore received the hearty approval of
military and chauvinistic circles in Japan, while
reports from Manchuria indicate that the Japanese there
welcome the appointment. The vernacular newspapers in
Japan generally voiced approval of the appointment, al-
though the OSAKA MAINICHI (in Jepanese) and the OSAKA
MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI-NICHI (in English) expressed
a note of doubt:
"But as an official of the S.M.R. what
achievements has Mr. Matsuoka to his credit?
His presence suggested virility and brilliance,
but what can be mentioned as concrete deeds
made possible solely through his ability?
One is tempted to raise this kind of question.
In spite of this, the fact that Mr. Matsuoka
enjoys extraordinary popularity may be
because his personality gives one the con-
stant expectation that he will surely do
'something'".
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 3, 1935, expressed
approval editorially of the appointment of Mr. Matsuoka,
who has staged, according to the newspaper, one of the
greatest "come-backs" in Japanese history. The news-
paper points out that the South Manchuria Railway
was formerly the politico-economic advance guard of
Japan in Manchuria, but that after the establishment of
"Manchukuo" the Railway became only a subsidiary economic
agency of Japanese power, requiring only a non-political
President

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.98)
37
-10-
President. The Railway, however, is now entering upon
a new phase and is likely to resume its former role of
politico-economic advance guard, but this time in North
China. This mission will require political-military
cooperation, and "if such a mission has to be fulfilled,
nobody is more able to fulfil it than Mr. Matsuoka".
It is reported, however, that the financiers of
Japan are not greatly pleased with Mr. Matsuoka's ap-
pointment. It appears that they are afraid that Mr.
Matsuoka, with his visionary ideas, backed by the
Kwantung Army, with equally or perhaps more visionary
ideas, may plunge the finances of "Manchukuo" and the
South Manchuria Railway into such difficulties that the
Japanese backers of enterprises in Manchuria will be
faced with ruin. It is reported that the South Man-
churia Railway will need about Yen 300,000,000 to finance
its new projects in Manchuria and North China. The full
amount cannot be raised by the issuance of new debentures,
as the Railway has almost reached the legal limit of its
debenture issue. Moreover, the capital of the Railway
cannot be increased, because the Japanese Government,
which must, by the charter of the Railway, own fifty
per cent of the stock, cannot take up its share of any
increased stock issue because of the strained condition
of Japanese Government finances. The Japanese financiers
are afraid of this situation, and fear that Mr. Matsuoka,
with his Army affiliations, may take some action which
w111

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.98)
138
-11-
will have a disastrous effect upon Japanese finances.
The Army, moreover, at first opposed the appearance
of "big business" on the Manchurian scene, although
later it found that it was necessary to make some con-
cessions to the financiers in order to obtain capital
for Japanese enterprises in Manchuria. The Japanese
financiers resent this attitude of the Army, and like-
wise resent the appointment of any man who is sympathetic
with Army views as President of the largest Japanese
corporation on the Asiatic mainland.
Mr. Matsuoka, in his press interviews, did not
comment upon the South Manchuria Railway "mission"in
North China. He expressed the opinion that the Railway
should now devote itself entirely to economic matters,
paying no attention to political matters; that the first
consideration should be the increasing of the economic
power of the Manchurian farmers, who comprise 90 per
cent of the population, as this would increase the earn-
ing power of the Railway; that forestry, mining and
cotton-raising should be encouraged in Manchuria; and
that it would be a mistake to encourage manufacturing
industries in Manchuria, as such would waste Japanese
capital, exert pressure upon Japanese industries, and
bring little benefit to the farmers of Manchuria.
Respectfully yours,
Ederin I. Jerill
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

Artieles of Assooiation of koohu Kungssu
Artiole I.
The corporation shall be called the kochu kungssu,
a joint stock oompany limited.
Artiole II.
The cap1tal of the corporation shall be 10,000,000
yen, Japanese currenoy.
Article III.
The main offices of the corporation shull be located
at Dairen.
Article Iv.
The corporation with a view to bringing closer
•conomio relations between Manchuria and China shall
be engaged mainly in the following lines of business:
(日)
Export and import trade with China,
agency and brokerage therefor.
(b)
Direot management of various economic
enterprises in China, mediation in the com-
moncement by other parties thereof and in-
vestments therein.
(0)
Business relating to or associated with
the foregoing.
Artiolo VI.
The stook of the corporation shall be divided into
200,000 shares with a face-value of 50 yen each.
Artiole VII.
All the shares of the oorporation shall be registered,
divided into four kinds of 1, and blocks of 10, lo0 and
1,000 shares.
Artiele VIII.

Artiole VIII.
The initial payment of capital on the shares shall
be 12.5 yen eaoh. As regards the sums and date of the
second and subseyuent payments, the shareholders shall
be notified thereof within 30 days of the announcement
thereof after determined by a meeting of shareholders.
Artiolo XVI.
The regular general meetings of shareholders shall
take plao• In June and December annually. Special
meetings shall be convened whenever they are deemed
necessary.
Artiole XXI.
The board of directors of the corporation shall
be composed of less than seven di rectors and less
than three auditors.
Artiole XXII.
The directors and auditors shall be elected from
among the holders of more than lo0 shares at generel
meetings of the shareholders.
Artiele XXIII.
The term ofoffice of the di rectors shall be three
years and that of the auditors two years.
Article XXVI.
One chairman shall be eleoted from among the
directors. The chairman is to represent the ∞rpor-
ation and supervise the business thereof.
Artiole xXIX.
The accounts of the corporation shall be settled
twice a year, namely, on Maroh 31 and September 30.
Article xxx.
From the gross profit of each term shall be sub-
tracted the total expenditures, the remaining amount
to be known as the profit for the term. To this
profit may be transferred the profit carried over from
the previous term and the total profit shell be dis-
posed of as follows:
(1)
5 per cent of the profit of the period
shall be applied to the legal reserve.
(8) 5 per

rent period shall be applied to the special
reserve.

A certain amount shall be applied to

staff.
(4)
l0 por oent of the profit of the cur-
rent poriod shall be applied to employees
bonus.
(5)
A oertain amount shnll he set eside for

(6)

to the fol lowine period.
Supplementary Article
Directors shall not serve for more than six terms,
and auditors shall not serve for more than four terms.
They shall be chosen at the annual meeting of the stook-
holders of the company.

Dairen's confidential despatch of
June 19, 1936, to the Embassy, Tokyo,
in regard to the relation of the Kwantung
Army to the South Manchuria Railway
Company.
The despatch refers to the funda-
mental conflict of interest between the
Kwantung Army and the South Manchuria
Railway Company, a matter which was the
subject of comment in Dairen's despatch
of April 6, 1936, and reports that the
threat of absorption of that company by
the Kwantung Army authorities was brought
to the attention of the public in a
recent press report (copy enclosed)
which outlines a drestic plan for the
reorganization of that company. This
plan is stated to comprise the following
points: (1) "Manchukuo" to be allowed
to take part in supplying capital to the
railway; (2) the railway to place itself
under the administretive rule of "Man-
chukuo"; (3) the rallway company to take
part in economic activities in north
China. The despatch points out that the
implementing of this plan would involve
(1) the formation of a new company, over
which the South Manchuria Railway Compeny
woula

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)

would have no control, to carry on ite
many commercial enterprises not connected
with communications; (2) the restriction
of the South Manchuria Railway Company's
activities to transportation; (3) the
transfer of the public service enter-
prises, such as Manchuria Telephone,
Telegraph Company and Manchuria Electric
Company to "Manchukuo", as well as (its
controlled) newspapers; (4) the control
from Hsinking through the General Affairs
Board of the Kwantung Bureau and the
communications division of the Kwantung
Army of the South Manchuria Railway
Company.
The despatch mentions that there 1s
much opposition on the part of employees
and officials of the railway company to
military dictation and expresses the
opinion that the President of the company
is far from being as confident of the
future of the company as his statements
would indicate.
The despatch states also that according
to information given a journalist by the
President of the company the South Manchuria
Railway Company and the State railways of
"Manchukuo" will be further unified in the
autumn and that the President declared
that within ten years time both entities
will form part of the "seme company".

OUTLINE OF PLAN PROYOSED BY TH ARMY
FOR REORGANILATION OF STRUCTORE OF
SOUTH MANCHURIA R.ILNAY COLP NY
Hatlonelity of the Compeny
Fer the most part, the Army is of the opinion that
the South Manehuria Railway Company shall be organized
as a Japanese juridicial person, but this does not apply
to the subsidiary companies whose status wil! be dis-
oussed separately.
Objests of the Company
The South Manchuria vailway Coap ny shall  dminister
roilways and harbors in m:anohukuo" and orth Chosen us
woll as incidental enterprises pertaining thereto.
The South Manohurie Railwey Comp ny will carry out
such enterprises as are ordered by the Goverment and
speelally it must reoognize that investments in lorth
China must be cerried out on e larger sc le then at
present.
Capital Structure of the Coupany
The present oapital of the South Manohurie Railwey
Company of Yen 800 000,000 shall be 1noreased tc Yen
1.000,000.000. or th1s Yen 200,000,000 inerease,
"Manehukuo" und the North Manchuria Railway (the former
Chinese Eastern Railway) shall contribute Yen 130,000,000,
aná the remaining Yen 70,000, 000 will be made up by the
contribution of property and e uipment by the "Manohukuo"
State Railwey. (The details of this contribution must
of course be left to political negotiations.)
The umpaid portion of the capital allotted to the
Japanese Government shall be made up by contribution of
oquipment and property of the Vorth Chosen Railway.
Such being the case plans shall be made for the unifi-
cation of the Manohukuo" and North Chosen Railweys.
As regards the limitation on the amount of debentures
which can be issued by the South Manchuria Railway Company
an Imperial Reseript has recently been issued whioh pro-
vides that such debentures must be limited to twiee the
amount of the paid up capital.
Dotaile

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.98)
Detaile of the South Manchurie Railway Company Enterprises
The management of railways and harbors shall be
umified umder the South Manchurie Reilway Company.
The Fushun Colliery and the Manchuria Collieries
shall be unified and placed under the administration of
the Manohukuo" Coal Administretion.
A now independent company shall be esteblished to
carry on the various commercial enterprises now under
the management or control of the south Manchuria Railway
Company.
Regulation of Subsidiary Companies
Traffio enterprises end those incidental thereto
shall remain under the control of the South Manchuria
Railway Company. This includes such enterprises as
railways, herbors, the Dairen 1een Faisha (Dairen
Steamship Company) and the anchuria arehouse Company.
Investments in orth Chine, other than in the
Shantung Iron orks nd the Feihan Collieries in which
the Company has already invested, shall be left for
further consideration.
The Showa Iron .orks of nshan sholl be placed under
the control of the ippon steel orks and other large
national trusts shall be under the dministration of
Japanese trusts of sir ilar nature.
The follov ing enterprises shall be turned over to
Manchukuo":
1. The Manchurie Telephone and Telegraph Company.
2. The Menchuria lectric Company.
3. All newapapers now controlled by the South
Manchuria Railwey Company.
The final disposition of such special enterprises
as the shale oil and coal liguefaction plants must be
the subjeot of further study.
A separate company shall be esteblished to operate
and control the lands and hotels now under the 3outh
Manchuria Railway Company.
Half of the stook of other subsidiary companies not
mentioned above shall be offered for public subscription.
Rogulation of Special Rights of the Company
The right of the South Manchuria Railway Company to
tax exemption will be abolished. At the same time the
payments by the South Manehuria Railway Company of special
dividends to the Government will be totally abolished.

- 3 -
Generel Superintendence of the South Manchuria Railway
In regard to the general superintendence of the
South Manchuria Railway Company, this has in general
been reformed at the time of the revision of the ad-
ministration of the Kwantung Leased Territory and the
Railway Zone under the Kwantung Bureawn, but in order
to make stronger the administrative unity between
Manehukuo" and Japan, the functions of the General
Administrative Board of the Kwantung Bureau and of
the Communications Section of the Kwantung rmy, which
at present allow both organizations some control over
the Railway, must be rationalized.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 19, 1936
Mr. Grummon at Dairen has written an
able and comprehensive report (40 pages)
on the balance sheet of the South Manchuria
Railway Company. The chief criticism one
can make of this report is that it is too
full and too detailed. The following is
not an attempt at summarizing; a few of the
more interesting facts are set forth:
The fiscal year ended April 1, 1936,
returned a record breaking profit to the
SMR of Yen 49,620,000. Total assets have
increased by about 10 to Yen 1,983,000,000.
To provide for expansion of the
Company's already dominating role in Man-
churia, President Matsuoka has elaborated a
"five-year plan", whereby it is proposed to
raise by the issue of debentures a sum of
approximately Yen 436,000,000 to prosecute
various enterprises in Manchuria and in
north China. However, the Company has
recently encountered some difficulty in
marketing its debentures, largely because
of fear by the investing public of
military pressure on a commercial
enterprise.
The

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
The SMR has advanced a large sum,
approximately Yen 540,000,000, to
"Manchukuo" for railroad purposes. This
amount remains unpaid and interest on it
also remains unpaid. When to this amount
is added the large sums invested in
unprofitable subsidiary companies largely
at the instance of tre military, it will be
understood why the investing public in
Japan has shown considerable reluctance to
take up the Company's debentures. There
is considerable speculation fhut the
Company will continue to exist as a profit
making organization, or become an official
agency such as the railways in Japan. At
present the directors are forced to steer
a difficult course between the demands of
"national policy" and sound business
policy.
At the end of the report is appended
a long list of the subsidiary companies
and affiliated enterprises of the SMR
Company. I suggest that you have a look
at this sheet, which gives a graphic
picture of the amazing extent of the
Company's control over industry in
Manchuria.
WTT/DLY

I have the honor to report on the budget of the
South Manchuria Railway Company for the fiscal year
28
onding March 31, 1939, which was announoed on March
17, l938, following approval by the Japanese Govern-
2
9
ment on the previous day of the estimates contained
RECORD NG
mont of the budget is appended to this report.
mterprise Budget
The Company's enterprise budget calls for the ex-
penditure of Yen 236,056,000 (Items A, B and C of the
enclosure), which represents an increase of about 20
million yen over last year's estimate of Yen £17,781,
FILED
o00. as shown in the enclosure, the modest sum of
Yen 69,520,000 (Items A and B) has been budgeted for
F/A
the ordinary and special enterprises of the Company it-
self, this sum being about 3 million yen in excess of
the analagous estimate for last year. The first item,
"Ordinary

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8-
"Ordinary Enterprises", estimated at Yen 31,249,000
(Item A), represents repairs and improvements to the
railroad and the Company's properties.
The second item, "Special Enterprises", estimated
at Yen 38,271,000 (Item B) comprises those undertaken
by the Company in accordance with the five-year in-
dustrial plan of Manchukuo". Among those enterprises
is the plan of increasing at a cost of Yen 15,019,000
(Item B.1) the output of the Showa Steel Works which
apparently it has not yet been deemed advisable for the
Company to hand over to the anchuria Heavy Industry
Development Company. Another special enterprise which
requires more money this year than last is the develop-
ment of the Fushun Coal Mines at a cost of Yen 8,379,000
(1tem B.3) as compared with yen 2,669,000 last year.
But on the other hand less is to be expended on exten-
sion of the Fushun Power Plant (Yen 3,741,000 - Item B.2 -
as against Yen 10,86l,000). The development of the coal
liquefaction plant which last year called for the ex-
penditure of over l0 million yen has apparently been in-
cluded this year in "Other" special enterprises, and re-
ference to Table I of the enclosure (which, however,
classifies expenditure on a somewhat different basis from
that of items B and c) shows that very little is to be
expended in that direction during the present year. It
has not been possible to ascertain whether this curtail-
ment indicates that the coal liquefaction enterprise has
proved unsatisfactory.
Far greater than the sum which the Company is to
spond

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
● 3 -
spend on its own enterprises is that appearing under
the third item: "Expenditures in Enterprises Outside of
the Company" amounting to Yen 166,537,000 (Item c).
The greater portion of this expense is to be applied
toward building new railways in "Manchukuo" - Yen 73,
889,000 (Item C.1), and toward improving existing lines
in that country - Yen 77,328,000 (Item C.2). The total
of these two items, Yen 151,z17,000 (Item C.l and C.2),
greatly erceeds the analagous estimates of last year,
Yen 103,964,000. It is safe to say that much of this
expenditure is attributable more to the strategic re-
quirements of the Kwantung Army than to normal economio
developments*, and it is probable that similar expendi-
tures will continue to appear in future budgets until
completion of the "Manchukuo" five-year railway build-
ing program. This program is variously estimated to
require 600 to 750 million yen.
The third item under the same heading is that
described as "Investments in Related Companies" - Yen
15,319,000 (Item C.3). This item shows a marked de-
cline from last year's estimate of Yen 46,850,000,
which is explained by the transfer to the Manchuria
Heavy Industry Development Company of many of the Com-
pany's enterprises. The transferred enterprises are
as follows.
Showa Steel Works
*The same is partially true of expenditures under item
B - "special Company Expenditures" which represent
the company's efforts to do its part under the
anchukuo" five-year industrial plan.

Plan of Finance

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.97)
• 5 -
Plan of Finance
Ordinary Company expenditures (Yen 31,z49,000 -
Item A) will be defrayed from reserve funds on hand.
Special Company expenditures and outside enterprises
(yen 204,607,000 - Items B and c) will be financed by
calling up Yen zO million on partly unpaid shares
held by the Japanese Government; by floating deben-
tures for Yen 170 million; and by "Other Means".
Since the total of items B and C is Yen 204 million,
the "Other Means" will have to bring in nearly Yen 15
million.
A more detailed account of the plan of
financing follows in the latter part of this report.
It is to be noted that it seems no longer possible for
the Company to call up capital from partly unpaid shares
held by the publie.
Estimated Business Revenues and Expenditures
were the South Manchuria Railway Company operated
as an ordinary business, its profits would be exceedingly
large.
The constant financial difficulties in which the
Company finds itself are of course the consequence of its
double role as a profit-making concern and as an instru-
ment of Empire.
Revenues for the budget year are estimated at Yen
316,275,000, a gain of Yen 36,807,000 over those of the
previous year. Expenditures are estimated at Yen 26l,
613,000, an increase of Yen 12,681,000 over those of
last year. The balance estimated at Yen 54,66z,000,
represents the Company's profits and is approximately
4 million yen more than estimated in the previous budget.
二

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
As in previous years, it is principally the opera-
tion of the railway itself which makes the surplus
possible, though coal mining, harbors and oil production
are also shown as profit making enterprises. The esti-
mated profit on the railway, at Yen 88,359,000, is
approximately Yen 600,O00 less than that for the previous
year.
The hotels will show a small loss, as usual, and
the vague and comprehensive items headed "General Affairs",
"Local Affairs" and "Industrial Grants" will require a
considerable expenditure in excess of revenue. In view
of the large profit accruing to the Company, a continua-
tion of the customary dividend of 8 per cent seems assured.
It is impracticable at this time to attempt to draw
conclusions from a comparison of the budget estimates for
the fiscal year recently ended with the actual results
of operation of the Company's enterprises during that
period inasmuch as the annual Report of the Company will
not be available until later this year.
It is obvious that certain adjustments in the basic
budget will have to be made in respect of procurement of
funds and in respect of the balance between income and
expenditure. For example, in this budget no account is
taken of the proceeds of shares of enterprises transferred
to the Manchuria Heavy Industry Development company and
of funds involved in the rendition to "Manchukuo" last year
of the former South Manchuria Railway Zone. Nor is there
any account taken of the widely disoussed plan of the South
Manchuria Railway Company to assume extensive new respon-
sibilities in North China, which, according to recent
statements

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
statements by Mr. Matsuoka, may absorb Yen l00 million
annually of the Company's funds for a period of five
years.
Financing
Company's
Despite the/favorable showing as a business concern,
the shares of the South Manchuria Railway Company are
no longer regarded by the public as gilt-edged investments,
largely on account of the Company's enforced subserviance
to military requirements. Considerable apprehension has
been expressed in recent months over the financial future
of the Company. However, the problem of finance appears
to have been solved for the current fiscal year by reason
of an easy money market and because of the proceeds of
the transfer to the Manchuria Heavy Industry Development
Company of shares of subsidiary enterprises.
As previously indicated, funds required for the Com-
pany's enterprises are estimated at Yen z36 million. As
a result of the Company's negotiations in Tokyo, it was
decided to supply these funds in accordance with the
following plan*:

TRANSLATION
BUDGET OF THE SOUTH MANCHURIA RAILWAY COMPANY
FOR THE FISCAL YEAR ENDING MARCH 31, 1939
The South Manchuria Railway Company's Budget for
1938-59 was approved today (i.e. March 16th) by the
Government. Reflecting conditions in Manchuria, the
enterprise budget for the period provides for the ut-
most retrenchment so far as the ordinary enterprises
of the Company are concerned, but, while endeavoring
to strengthen the Company's finances, necessary special
enterprises have been adopted, to the limit permitted
by finance, after consideration of their prospects for
profit. The principal items are as follows:-

1 have the honor to transmit herewith, in the form
of an appendix to this despatch, a synopsis of the fi-
nancial report for the fiscal year ended March 31, 1938,
6
presented by the South Manchuria Railway Company at the
37th Annual General Meeting of Shareholders held at
Tokyo on June 20, 1938, and to submit the following com-
ments in regard thereto.
1t should be mentioned in passing that the task of
the student of the Company's finances has not been made
easier since the outbreak of Sino-Japanese hostilities.
As the Company assumes more and more the role of an in-
F/A
strument of empire on the continent and as it becomes
increasingly subservient to governmental - that is to
say military - control, the balance sheet becomes cor-
respondingly unreliable as an index to the true finan-
cial position of the Company. Especially evident is
the omission of data that might be conceived as having
strategic
11

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
strategic importance.

assets and liabilities of the Company balanced at Yen
2,243,194,000. The various items are s et forth in
outline on pages 1 and 2 of the Appendix to this des-
paton.
