BUSINESS OPERATIONS (Enterprises)
During the 1937 fiscal year the Company's revenue
from its enterprises was ren 355,040,000 and expenditure
was ren 281,118,000, resulting in a profit balance of
Yen 73,929,000. Attention is invited to the Profit
and Loss Account set forth on page 3 of the Appendix,
and to the Business Revenue and Expenditure Account
on page 4 of the appendix.
The profit of nearly yen 74 millions is tne largest
returned in the thirty-seven years since the establishment
of the Company, and serves as evidence of the immense
money-making potentialities of the Company. Comparing
the results of the 1937 year with those of the pre-
ceding year, revenue showed great increase in the sum
of Yen 56 millions which was partially offset by an in-
crease in total expenditure in the sum of ren 32 millions.
The inorease in profit was therefore Yen 23,755,000.
Details under the headings of the several enterprises
are given below. Further remarks covering the financial
results of the year's operations will follow in General
Comments, below.

The railways constitute much the greatest source of

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.98)

revenues were obtained principally from freight traffic
Yen 115,560,000, and from passenger traffio Yen 24,875,

warehouses. The principal items of railway expendi-
ture were operation of trains Yen 12,930,000; trans-
portation Yen 9,670,000; repair and upkeep of vehicles
and trackage Yen 14,750,000 et cetera.
The increase in revenue from the railway, (yen 1l.8
millions) is accounted for in official announcements by
the establishment of peace and order in Manchuria which
has permitted the development of productive industry
and the great increase in passenger traffic in Manchu-
ria. An additional factor is the great increase of
movements of troops and material necessitated by Japan-
ese military operations on the continent.
The 1937 year was one of unprecedentedly profitable
operations for the railways. By way of comparison,
passengers carried during the 1937 year numbered 17,520,
ooo and the revenue from passenger traffic was Yen 24,
878,000, whereas the analogous figures for the preceding
year were Yen 15,750,000 and Yen 22,303,000 respeotively.
Freight transported last year amounted to 25,130,000
metrio tons and income from freight transportation
amounted to Yen 115,558,000, whereas in the preceding
year the comparable figures were 21,370,000 and Yen
103,165,000. Increases on the expenditure side are
accounted for principally by increased cost of opera-
tion and administration, including "protective equip-
ment on account of the Chima incident*. Depreoiation

largest item contributing toward the increase by Yen
23,750,000 of profit for 1937 over that for 1936 is the
increased profit shown under the heading of "General".
The latter is accounted for by the inclusion under this
heading of a "premium" amounting to Yen 30.5 millions
accruing to the Company from the transfer of shares in
certain subsidiaries to the Manchuria Heavy Industry
Company at a value fixed by an official commission in
excess

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
exeess of the book value. Were these funds omitted
from consideration, the profits from operation of the
railroad would stand out clearly as the major source
of the l937 year's profit. The railroad's revenue of
Yen 15l millions and expenditure of Yen 53 millions,
when compared with the previous year's figures of Yen
133 millions and Yen 48 millions, shows an increase
in profit by Yen ll.8 millions.
In view of the great war-time demand for coal and
in view of the fact that gross revenue from mines, con-
sisting mainly of coal mines, showed an increase by Yen
from mining operations
3.2 millions, it might seem strange that the net profit/
shows a decline from last year. However, this may be
explained by the fact that special efforts have been
made, by heavy assessments toward depletion (Yen 12
millions) and by other means to put the collieries on
a sound financial basis with a view to preparing against
a future rainy day.
Another point of unusual interest is that harbors
and wharves showed an increased profit over last year
by about Yen 1.2 millions. There has been considerable
criticism expressed on account of the inability of the
harbor facilities to cope with the present volume of
shipping and on account of the large profit of approxi-
mately Yen 5 millions returned by a semi-publio utility.
However, it may be argued that although Dairen has more
modern equipment than any other port in Japan, the
charges at this port are cheaper than at any other port
in Japan, and that the South Manchuria Railway Company
has

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.98)
● 13 -
has installed equipment worth many millions for which
no interest is charged.
DISPOSITION OF PROFITS
As shown on page 3 of the Appendix, the net profit
of the 1937 year's operations amounted to Yen 73,929,000
to which should be added the unexpended balance brought
forward from the 1936 year, Yen 17,467,000, making a
grand total of Yen 91,397,000 to be disposed of as the
results of the year's operations. The table in the
Appendix above-mentioned gives a sohedule of the dis-
position of profits decided on by the Directorate and
approved by the General Meeting.
In connection with this schedule of profit dis-
position it is noteworthy that in the face of a strong
public demand for a 10 per cent dividend on account of
the favorable financial showing during the fiscal year,
the Directorate set aside the sum of Yen 20 millions as
a special dividend equalization fund against a possible
rainy day. Yen 20 millions applied against the shares
outstanding in the hands of the public would finance a
dividend of 5 per cent. The shareholders have been
given to understand that in view of the general situa-
tion at present they need expect no increase in the cus-
tomary rate of dividend (8 per cent), but that they bene-
fit from the stabilizing effect of this fund on the value
of their shares, while at the same time the Company bene-
fits from the capital thus made available for its fi-
nancing operations.
It

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.97)
- 14 ●
It will also be noted that the sum of Yen 100,000
was allotted for increasing the bonuses of the direc-
tors. In 1927 and 1928 the allotment was Yen 650,000,
but in the depression years of 1930 and 1931 the sum
was reduced to Yen 400,000 where it has since remained.
Under present circumstances, and with the recent in-
creases in the number of directors, the increased
allotment may be regarded as reasonable.  However,
there will undoubtedly be grumbling on the part of
unchanged in the face of a rapidly increased cost of
living in Manchuria.

ENTERPRISES AND INVESTENTS

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.98)
- 16 
ENTERPRISES AND INVESTMENTS - COMMENT
There are frequently heard expressions of anxiety
over the financial future of the Company, not on account
of the ordinary business activities, but on account of
the huge sums expended annually in enterprises and in-
vestments outside of the Gompany at a time when capital
for such purposes is inoreasingly diffioult to obtain.
This anxiety is evidenced by the fact that the Company's
shares remain extremely low on the market* in spite of
the steady and generous dividends of 8 per cent. The
reason for the uneasiness of the investing publio is,
of course, the heavy and largely unexplained expenditures
under the heading of *development expenditures", and the
constant reports appearing in the press about projeoted
investments of the South Manchuria Railway Company in
industrial schemes in North China.
In this connection the following table is of interest:

market quotations of the South Manohuria Railway Company

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.97)
● 17 
The first figure, Yen 50,280,000, represents the
normal or "legitimate" expenditures of the Company in
developing its own enterprises, as shown in detail on
pages 5 and 6 of the Appendix. This sum is dwarfed
by the enormous sum of nearly Yen l64 millions expended
on extra-Company enterprises of whioh the Manchoukuo
State Railways required approximately Yen l22 millions*.
Although it appears that the State Railways are begin-
ning to show a profit, the fact remains that the in-
vestors are fearful of the consequences of using the
Company as an instrument of national polioy. The un-
certainty will remain until the competent authorities,
that is to say the Kwantung Army, make a definite
announcement in regard to the future of the State Rail-
ways and the relationship between those lines and the
South Manchuria Railway. The fact is, of course, that
the State Railways are, and will probably remain, the
property of the South Manchuria Railway for all intents
and purposes.
In striking contrast to the Yen 130 millions of
Company debentures soheduled for issuance during the
1957 year was the aotual sum found to be marketable,

Ten 20 millions, demonstrating again the reluctance of

Company. The unissued Yen 1lo millions will be added
to the Yen 170 millions soheduled for accomodation during
the 1938 year.
It appears, however, that the authorities of the
Railway are less concerned at this time about the problem
of raising capital, kmotty as that may be, than they are
about the problems arising out of new war-time laws con-
trolling such capital. It is generally felt that the
Government will provide the way for supplying necespary
funds to an organization which is so great a degree
FOR GUARD
instrument of the Japanese Government on the continents.
but on the other hand, recent laws have made it very
diffioult for the Company to make definite plans for
the future. However favorable the financial standing
of the Company may be, there remains the problem of ob-
taining materials and supplies, domestio and imported,
in regard to whioh the Company's needs are now largely
subordinated to military requirements. For example,
the funds allotted in the l937 budget for development
expenditures on the Company's own lines was approxi-
mately Yen 88 millions, whereas the sum actually ex-
pended was only Yen 50.2 millions, as shown in the
schedule of fixed assets on page 5 of the Appendir.
The balance, Yen 38 millions, remained unexpended and
npexpendible

FR GUARD
unerpendible for the purpose because of the inability
of the Company to obtain supplies under present condi-
tions. It is just this situation that adds a specula-
tive element to the South Manchuria Railway Company
Company's future and that depresses the Company's securi-
ties on the market.

the auditor of the South Manchuria Railway Company, and
my British Colleague.

entitled "Budget of the South Manohurie Railway

budget calls for an inoreased erpenditure of
188 million yen, by far the largest estimate
ever adopted by the company. The total
erpenditure for the 1939-1940 fisoal year
will be Yen 408,908,000, this 1noluding a
upplementary budget or Yen 50,000,000. It
would seem that in many instanoes the increase
in the budget is due to the inoreased oost of
matorials and supplies. Yen 99,000,000 1s to
be spent on the company's own enterprises but
the huge sum of Yen 260,000,000 1s to be spont
on "enterprises outside or the company". Mr.
Turner desoribes this amount as the "burden
whioh the oompany 1s called upon to bear in
1ts role as an instrument of national polioy".
The greater portion of the amount is for
oonstruotlon of now lines and double-traoking
of the "Manohukuo" state railways and toward
improving existing lines for strategio rather
than eoonomio requirenents. Yen 24,000,000
is to be 1nvested in the nowly organizod North
China Trafrio Company.
Mr. Turnor states that the impression
reoeived from the pross is that the company
does not find it easy to persuade the Japanese
Govornment

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

Government to pay its balance of uncalled
capital. It is stated that the company's
capitalization will have to be increased to
provide funds. Revenues from operation of the
company's enterprises are estimated at Yen
380,000,000, a large inorease over revenues in
fm preceding years. Mr. Turner states that,
were the South Manohuria Railway Company
operating as an ordinary business, its profits
would be exceedingly great but the constant
financial difficulties in which the company
finds itself are of course the consequence of
its double role as a profit-making concern and
as an instrument of Empire. A continuation
of the customary dividend of 8 percent seems
assured, and, in fact, the company has had
to resist strong demand on the part of the
shareholders for inoreased dividends.

TRANSATION
BUDGET OF THE SOUTH LANCHURIA RAILWAY CONPANY
FOR THE FISCAL YEAR EIDINC LARCH 31. 1940
The South Manohuriu Railway Company's budget for
the fisoal year 1939-40 (pril 1, 1939 - liarch 31, 1940)
was approved today (1.e. Laroh loth) by the Government.
While every effort has been made to postpono until the
future all enterprises thit c"n possibly be deferred,
yet the important role of the company in the industrial
development has madc it absolutel" necessary to inorease
the enterpriso bud et for the ensuing year beyond the

DÉPARTMENT OF SI TE
SIRI
1 huve the honor to transmit herewith, in the form
of an appendix to this despatcn, a synopsis of the fi-
nancial repnort for the fiscal year ended .arch 31, 1939,
presented by the South anchuria Railway Company at the
38th Annual General Meeting of Shareholders held at Tokyo
on June 20, 1939, and to submit the following comment in
regard thereto.
In a report made by this Consulate on the balance
sheet of the Company for the preceding (1937) fiscal year*
it was stated that the task of the student of the Company's.
finance has not been made easy since the outbreak of Sind-
Japanese hostilities. In the year since that despatch
F/WB
was written new laws and regulations designed to preserve
economic secrets have made it even more difficult to ob-
tain data in regard to the financial position of the Com-
pany. Very meager comment in regard to the annual fi-
nancial report of one of Japan's most important business
organizations
*consulate's despatch No. 47 of September 9, 1938, "Balance Sheet
of the South Manchuria Railway Company for the Fiscal Year
Ended March 31, 1938."

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
organizations has appeared either in the press or in fi-
nancial journals.
The Company's report itself is, of
course, in the barest outline with a minimum of ex-
planatory comment.
ASSETS
AND LIABILITIES
On March 31, 1939, at the end of the fiscal year
1938-39 (hereinafter called the 1938 fiscal year), the
assets and liabilities of the Company balanced at Yen
2,446,858,000. To balance the account there is shown
on the "liability" side of the ledger the net profit for
the 1938 year, Yen 72,875,000, and the balance brought
forward from the previous year, Yen 17,733,000.
The
various items in the balance sheet are set forth on pages
1 and 2 of the appendix to this despatch.
ASSETS
assets have more than doubled in the past eight years
as shown by the following table:
1931
Yen
1,154 million

1,245
1"

1,612
n

1,811

1,933
1936
2,109

2,243
1"
1938
2,446
The assets of the Company increased by Yen 203,664,000
during the 1938 fiscal year.
While a considerable portion
of this increase is due to extension and development of the
Company's own properties, a far greater portion represents
investments by the Company in the State Railways of
"Manchukuo"

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
Mamohukuo", those being the lines seized from the Chinese
and acquired from other sources, including the old Chinese
Eastern Railway.
A short discussion of the several items
of the Company's assets will follow, which will be more
easily comprehended if reference is made to the table of
assets on page 1 of the Appendix.
Subscribed Capital Stock Uncalled (Yen 103.792 O00)
All of the capital stock remaining uncalled is owned
by the Government, the shares held by the general public
having been fully paid up during 1937.
During the 1938
fiscal year the Government paid in Yen 20 million on its
shares, not without considerable urging by the Company,
and the remaining uncalled stock now amounts to Yen 103,
792,000.
Further aiscussion of these matters will be
found later in this despatch.
Fixed Assets (Yen 892.164.000)
A detailed schedule of the Company's fixed assets
will be found on page 5 of the Appendix, in which are shown
the sums allotted for developing such assets, transfers of
property, and the amounts assessed for depletion and depre-
ciation. During the 1938 year the sum of Yen 79 million
was spent on improvement and development, while deprecia-
tion of the properties was estimated at Yen 34 million and
inventory removals were Yen 5 million, leaving a net gain
in value of the fixed assets of Yen 39 million.
The balance sheet might lead one to believe that great
gains had been made in the valuation of the railways and
"miscellaneous" assets, but actually these increases are
largely accounted for by transfers of property such as
land, schools and hospitals which were formerly included
under

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
一4 -
under the heading of "local administration" and which were
supposed to have been largely handed over to "Manchukuo"
with the abolition of extraterritoriality. apparently
the Company intend to keep much of this property, al-
though according to an official of the Company some of
it will later be transferred.
A scrutiny of the schedule of fixed assets on page
5 of the appendix and of the schedule on page 6 of ex-
penditures in developing those assets reveals some in-
teresting facts.
The Company spent the modest sum of
Yen 22 million on its own railway lines; at the same
time it spent Yen 26 million on the development of its
collieries; Yen 10 million on its oil distillation
plant, and Yen 7 million on its coal liquefaction plant.
In other words the Company spent on the aevelopment of
fuel and fuel resources more than twice as much as it
did on the railway itself.
It is common knowledge
that the coal and oil resources of Manchuria are being
taxed to the utmost by the present exigencies in the
Far East.
It is also interesting to note that the
collieries which are valued at Yen 148 million have
assessed against them the extraordinarily large sum of
Yen 16 million for depreciation and depletion.
The items of hotels, industrial grants, and local
administration which appeared in previous balance sheets
of the Company have disappeared and the assets under these
headings have been transferred to other items: the hotels
are now part of the item of railways, and the local ad-
ministration property has been divided between the rail-
ways and "miscellaneous assets", as was mentioned above.
The

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
The assets of the small item of industrial grants can no
longer be identified.
Investments (Yen 919,335.000)
Investments have increased to a much greater extent
than any other asset
of the Company, having been in 1931
only Yen 157 million;
in 1935 Yen 543 million; and in
1938 Yen 919 million.
Investments increased since 1937
by about Yen 142 million.
Little change took place dur-
ing the past year in any of the several itens of invest-
ment except that headed "Loans to Lanchoukuo Government
Railways" which increased by Yen 134 million from Yen 613
million to Yen 747 million.
This item now represents by
far the largest asset of the Company and begins to ap-
proach in size the total value of all the physical pro-
perties of the Company itself.
The capital for these investments was raised in the
main by the flotation of debentures, which have increased
from Yen 315 million in 193l to Yen 95l million in 1938
and on which interest charges continue to mount.
Fur-
ther mention of this item will be made under "Unadjusted
Accounts", below. It is noted that the sum of Yen 9,
660,000 has been charged against the item of investments
on account of depreciation, and a further sum of Yen 2,
750,o00 charged as discount on bonds on account of de-
preciation.
Current Assets (Yen 285.277,000)
There is little of special interest under the head-
ing of current assets, the value of which has changed
but little since the preceding year. However, it may
be noted that while the Company's cash on hand dropped
by

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
by about Yen 44 million, the inventories of materials
and supplies has increased by about the same amount.
In view of its great difficulties in l937 in procuring
materials for normal operations it seems likely that the
Company has made special efforts to lay in large supplies.
It is known, for example, that the Company purchased last
year from an american oii company sufficient stocks of
lubricating oil to last for three years in the alleged
effort to provide against possible interruption of im-
ports from abroad.
Unad justed Accounts (Yen 246.288.000)
Under this heading are lumped all cash advances, un-
settled accounts and unpaid partial payments due the Com-
pany.
The item shows an increase by about Yen 43 million
since the previous year.
It is safe to say that all but
a small part of this account relates to materials and
services rendered to the "Manchukuo" State Railways.
Although the State Railways are managed and operated by
the Company their accounts have never been made public
and their only apparent financial connection with the
Company's account is in the item "Interest" in the pro-
fit and loss account.
LIABILITIES
Capital Stock Subscribed (Yen 800.000.000)
Last year after much negotiation and controversy
the Company succeeded in prevailing on the Japanese
Government to pay in Yen 20 million on its half of the
subscribed capital stock, making the uncalled balance on
the Government's shares Yen 103,792,000, out of a sub-
scribed

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
scribed total of Yen 800,000,000.
The half of the capi-
tal stock subscribed by the public had been entirely paid
up during the previous year.
The increasing demand for capital for use by the Com-
pany has compelled the directors to consider a revision
of the ordinances of the Company to permit increased
capitalization.
at the present time these ordinances
permit debentures to be issued in a sum twice the amount
of the paid up capital or to a possible maximum of Yen
1,600 million.
Debentures outstanding on arch 31, 1939,
were Yen 95l million, leaving an issuable balance of
about Yen 650 million.
It will easily be seen that,
at the present rate of expenuiture by the Company of
about Yen 360 million per annum, this balance will soon
disappear and the limit of debentures issuable reached.
Reserves (Yen 278.890,000)
The increase in the reserves of the Company by about
Yen 30 million is mainly accounted for by the new and in-
teresting item of "dividend reserve" - Yen 20 million,
which was set aside last year from the proceeds of the
sale of subsidiary firms as a saving against a possible
rainy day when profits will not be as easy as they are at
present.
The legal reserve, which by law must be at
least one-twentieth of the annual net profit, exceeds
the minimum by a considerable margin.
The special re-
serve, which by law must be at least one-seventh of the
annual net profit, is actually almost three times the
amount of that profit.
Chargeable against this reserve
are retirement and dismissal allowances of employees.
Bonds

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
Bonds (Yen 951.825.000)
a list of the debenture issues outstanding on Larch
31, 1939 appears on page 8 of the appendix.
During the
fiscal year 1938 the total of debentures issued was Yen
220,000,000.
After retiring several early issues of
debentures, the net increase in debentures oustanding
over those of the previous year was Yen 153,875,000.
It is interesting that the interest rate on these issues,
which during 1936 and 1937 had dropped to 4.2 and 4.1,
has again risen to 4.3.
During the 1938 year interest
payments on the Company's debentures amounteu to about
Yen 37 million.
Under the ordinances of the Company the limit of
debentures issuable is twice the anount of the paid-up
capital, i.e., twice Yen 696,208,000 or Yen 1,392,416,000
as of March 31, 1939.
Thus the balance issuable against
this limit was Yen 440,591,000 at the end of the fiscal
year.
Current Liabilities (Yen 95.035.000)
Under this heading there appears little of interest.
The total has decreased since the preceding year by about
Yen 15 million, chiefly because the amount of bills pay-
able has been substantially reduced.
Deposits (Yen 199,166,303,000)
Deposits, consisting largely of employees'savings
and sureties, show a substantial increase by about Yen 27
million.
Deposits by subsidiary companies also show an
increase.
Unadjusted

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9-
Unadjusted Accounts (Yen 31,332, 724)
This account, which is a sort of catch-all for
sundry unsettled receipts, shows a small increase by
about Yen 9 million over that of the previous year.

The railways as usual constitute much the greatest
source of the Company's revenue.
The enormous profit of
Yen 109 million which far exceeds that of any previous
year serves as evidence of the great money-making poten-
tialities of the railway were it free to operate as an
ordinary business.
Revenues were obtained principally
from freight traffic, Yen 135,089,000, and from passenger
traffic

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
traffic, Yen 33,940,000, other revenues being derived
from warehouses and dining cars as well as the hotels
which have been included in the railway account.
The increase in revenue from the railway, by nearly
Yen 15 million, is accounted for in official announce-
ments by the progress of industrial development in Man-
churia, by the restoration of peace in contiguous terri-
tories of North China, by the reat increase in travel
by tourists and businessmen and by the heavy traffic in
building material and supplies.
an additional factor
is the great amount of traffic of troops and military
supplies necessitated by Japanese military operations
on the continent.
The 1938 year was one of unprecedentedly profitable
operations for the railways.
By way of comparison, pas-
sengers carried durin: the 1938 year numbered 21,030,000
and the revenue from passenger traffic was Yen 33,940,000,
whereas the analogous figures for the preceding year were
17,520,000 passengers and Yen z4,878,000 respectively.
Freight transported in the 1938 year amounted to 26,420,
ooo metric tons and income from freight transportation
was Yen 135,889,000, whereas in the previous year the
comparable figures were 25,130,000 metric tons and Yen
115,558,000.
Increases on the expenditure side are accounted for
in official pronouncements by the increased cost of ad-
ministration and by the creat increase in costs of ma-
terials.
The cost of all commodities, including labor,
have shown marked appreciation during the past two years
in Manchuria.

tions during the 1938 fiscal year there was a strong de-
mand on the part of the public for a 10 per cent dividend.
However, the Company made it known that in view of the
special conditions prevailing at the present time the
shareholders need expect no increase in the customary
rate of dividend (8 per cent).
The portion of the profit distributed outside of the
Company in the form of dividends and bonuses to officials
amounted to Yen 45,40l,000 whereas the funds retained in
the Company as reserves and as a balance carried forward
amounted

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
-17 -
amounted to Yen 45,207,000. The large sum of Yen 26,
557,o00 was carried forward as a balance to the follow-
ing year in conformity with usual Japanese accounting
practice. This sum is more than one-third of the net
profit. It is declared by one commentator that the
large size of the balance carried forward is an indica-
tion that the Company was unable to obtain the materials
and labor for which funds had been provided.
GENERAL COMMENT ON THE CONPANY'S FINANCES
The very considerable appreciation in value during
the past 12 months of the Company's shares on the market,
despite the absence of dividend increase, is commonly
pointed to as evidence of increasing public confidence
in the financial future of the South Manchuria Railway
Company*.
Certainly to the American observer a share
in a semi-official firm which has the vast assets and
successful history of the Company, and which has regularly
paid a dividend of 8 per cent, would seem a tremendous
bargain at Yen 70. The fact is, however, that the appre-
ciation in the Company's shares is little if any greater
than the increase in general property and security values
brought about by prevailing inflationary forces, and there
are factors which continue to inhibit optimism concerning
the Company's financial future.
The
*As of interest in this connection there has been prepared a
table, shown on page 9 of the Appendix, in which appear
market quotations of the South Manchuria Railway Company
since April 1938. There is also shown, on page 10 of the
Appendix, a list of some of the largest shareholders taken
from a Company publication, which has never before been
published as far known.

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
The real reason for the uneasiness of the investing
public is, of course, the fear of the ultimate consequences
to the Company of its use by the Kwantung Army as an in-
strument of national policy.
The prevailing emergency in
Japanese affairs on the continent makes increasingly heavy
demands of a military character on the South Manchuria Rail-
way Company.
The Company has been forced to contribute in
large measure its facilities, funds and personnel to mili-
tary objectives while at the same time it has had to ex-
pand its facilities in order to cope with plans for greatly
increased industrial development in anchuria also spon-
sored by the army.
The cost of these activities, as re-
flected in the Company's balance sheet, give rise to feel-
ings of uncertainty among the investing public.
A year ago it was announced in a Company publication
that the total investment by the Company in the State Rail-
ways was Yen 8l0 million*.
It seems very likely that there
has been an increase in this investment in about the same
proportion as the increase in the two items headed "Loans
to Manchoukuo Government Railways" and "Unadjusted accounts"
(Yen 142 million plus Yen 43 million).
In other words it
is estimated that the South anchuria Railway Company now
has invested in or loaned to the State Railways the truly
huge sum of about 995 million.
This estimate was par-
tially confirmed in a conversation between the writer and
one of the auditors of the Company.
It is realized by
everyone that much or most of these funds have been spent
in the construction of new lines which are of military
rather
*Page 3 of Consulate's despatch No. 47 of September 9, 1938,
Balance Sheet of the South Manchuria Railway Company for
the Fiscal Year Ended March 31, 1938.

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
rather than economic value and which may never return a
profit.
In this connection the following table showing the
Company's budget estimate of development expenditure for
the current year is of interest:
Development Expenditure - 1939
Expenditures within the Company
* 99,041,000
Expenditures outside of the Compuny
State Railways - construction
¥119,707,000
State Railways - repair
89,380,000
Investment in subsidiaries
50.780.000
259.867,000
￥358,908,000
The first figure, Yen 99 million, represents the normal
or "legitimate" expenditures of the Company in developing
its own enterprises.
This sum is dwarfed by the enormous
sum of Yen 259 million to be expended on extra-Company
enterprises of which the "Manchukuo" State Railways re-
quire Yen z09 million million. Although it is claimed
that the State Railways are beginning to show a profit,
the fact remains that the shareholders are kept completely
in the dark as to the accounts of those lines and have no
reliable information whether the Company's investments in
those lines, shown as assets in the Company's report and
appraised at about Yen 995 million, are actually worth a
fraction of that sum. Uncertainty in regard to the fi-
nancial condition of the Company will remain until the
competent authorities, that is to say the Kwantung Army,
make public the accounts of the State Railways and announce
definitely the future relationship between those lines and
the South Manchuria Railway Company.
But an announcement
of the accounts of the State Railways will probably have
to

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
-20 -
to await a decision in regard to the much-discussed pro-
blem of increased capitalization of the South lanchuria
Railway Company which is closely bound up with the matter
of investment in the State Railways, as will be discussed
below.
How the increasingly heavy obligations of the Com-
pany are to be financed at a time when the Government and
the investing public show little inclination to provide
further capital is the major problem confronting the di-
rectors. There has been some talk of a revision of the
Company's ordinances to permit increasing its capitaliza-
tion from the present Yen 800 million to Yen 1,200 million
or even Yen 1,600 million in order to solve the problem of
funds. However the difficulty attending this pronosal is
the apparent inability of the Government under present con-
ditions to raise its share of the "ante". Illustrating
this difficulty are the following remarks attributed by
the press to Mr. Lutsuoka, the outspoken former president
of the South Manchuria Railway Company on Kovember 26, 1938:
"The attitude of the Government, which keeps
loading on to the South Manchuria Railway the du-
ties of an instrument of its policy but which with-
holds funds for its financing, is extraordinary
indeed. I have come over (to To. yo) to state the
Company's case strongly to the Finance Minister and
other officials. The sum (which was lopped from
the payment demanded) ... isn't so very big from
the standpoint of the Company, but in view of the
103 million yen unpaid by the Government on its
capital shares I think that the attitude of the
Government toward the matter is most peculiar.
While they call us an instrument of national
policy and load all kinds of burdens on us they
treat us like this!"
It is rumored that an alternate proposal for raising
oapital has been advanced which would permit the "Manchukuo"
Government to invest in the Company. A discussion of this
proposal

=== Page 354 === (confidence 1.00)
-21-
proposal in a recent issue of a local publication was
inked out, presumably by police orders, but the Consulate
was able to determine that the proposal would involve
"investment in kind" by the "Manchukuo" Government.
In
other words "Manchukuo" would contribute as its share of
capital in lieu of funds the properties seized from the
former Chinese railways and those acquired from the for-
mer Chinese Eastern Railway. It is reported, however,
that the Tokyo authorities are cool to the idea of per-
mitting the "Manchukuo" Government to own a large share
in an important "national policy" company.
In conclusion it may be mentioned that the informa-
tion contained in this reoort was obtained fror the annual
Report for 1938 of the South Manchuria Railway Company,
the local press, the anshu hyron (magazine), the "Diamond"
(magazine), and officials of the South Lianchuria Railway
Company.

William T. Turner

LEGAL ADVISER
193
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 9, 1939.
JE:
Reference Peiping's despatch no. 2226
of August 22 in regard to certain locomotive
parts now held at Hong Kong by the American
President Lines and under consignment to the
Peiping-Suiyuan Railway . .
Bearing in mind that the Department has
approved of direct approaches to the Japanese
authorities by our Embassies at Peiping and
Tokyo in regard to matters relating to
Chinese railways in north China in which there
exist American interests, it may be that there
was no impropriety in the action of our Embassy
at Peiping in complying with the request of
the American President Lines that the subject
of the non-delivery of the goods in question
be brought to the attention of the Japanese
Embassy. However, although it is by no means
clear in whom titie to the goods in question
is now vested, the fact that the goods are
consigned to "ORDER BANk OF CHINA NOTIFY
PEIPING-SUIYUAN RAILWAY, PEIPING", both of
which organizations are directly connected
with the Chinese Government, would seem to
render advisable consideration on the part of
the American President Lines of the question
of bringing the case under discussion to the
attention of the appropriate authorities of
the Chinese Government at Chungking. In con-
sequence, the attached telegram to Hong Kong
has been drafted along the lines indicated.
reeu vn
FE:Mackay:JPS

Enclosure No. 1
Despatch No. 2226.
(CORRECT COPY - SC)
AMERICAN PRESIDENT LINES
Hong Kong, August 4 1939.
Embassy of the United States of America
Peiping,North China
Gentlemen:
Undelivered cargo ex ss President Monroe 44
We attach a copy of a letter dated June 5th addressed
by us to the North China Bureau, South Manchuria Railway,
Tientsin, which speaks for itself, to which we regret to
say we have not been able to secure a reply. We understand
this is probably due to the hostilities in North China,
and inasmuch as the North China Bureau, South Manchuria
Railway, are now under control of Japan, we would like
to request that yau be good enough to communicate with
the Japanese Embassy in your City in the matter, request-
ing their co-operation to see tnat our letter is replied
to by the proper party.
This cargo has been on hand at Hong Kong for a
considerable time, and storage charges outstanding on
this shipment now run in the neighborhood of 600.00, and
we are naturally anxious to liquidate the shipment from our
records. The party claiming delivery will have to present
to us the accomplished original Bill. of Lading showing
title to the goods.
Your co-operation in his matter will be greatly
appreciated.
Very truly yours,
W. F. Arndt
W. F. Arndt
Freight Agent

Despatch No. 2226
AUGUST 19, 1939.)
MEMORANDUM
Peiping, August 18, 1939.
Subject:
Shipment of locomotive parts
assigned to "Order Bank of China
Notify Peiping-Suiyuan Railway,
Peiping."
There is attached a copy of a letter, under date June
5, 1939, addressed by Mr. W. F. Arndt, Freight Agent of
the American President Lines at Hong Kong, to he North China
Bureau, South Manchuria Railway, in regard to a shipment
of locomotive parts assigned to "Order Bank of China
Notify Peiping-Suiyuan "ailway, Peiping". Mr. Arndt
has informed this Embassy that no reply has been received
to his letter.
In view of the fact that the cargo has now been on
hand in Hong Kong for a considerable time and the fact
that the storage charges outstanding on this shipment amount
to approximately $60o, it is requested that the Japanese
Embassy transmit the substance of this memorandum and of its
enclosure to the appropriate autnorities with the suggestion
that a reply be forwarded to the American President Lines.
Enclosure:
Copy of Mr. Arndt's letter
of June 5, 1939.
